You have touched a point of considerable importance, one I have long pondered.

I am inclined to believe we must alter our notion of electromagnetic induction in a cavity of thermal equilibrium. We must understand that the energy emerging from such a cavity is not a continuous stream of modes as we might suppose an ordinary field to be, but rather a series of separate modes each capable of seizing energy in varying quantities according to frequency. Each mode emerges at a particular frequency, and then passes through its peak and then elsewhere, thereby generating an infinite total energy.

Now, I observe that at higher frequencies, the energy does not fall uniformly along a continuous line, but rather shifts into multiple modes in succession. Each mode takes up energy at varying frequencies, and the total energy is finite. We might argue that each mode requires energy in definite quantity, and that the process is not continuous but rather broken up, each mode being excited at a different frequency.

This differs markedly from an ordinary electromagnetic field where the field distributes itself uniformly throughout a volume. The energy emerges in continuous stream, and we might expect to find a higher maximum at a higher frequency. The experiment I cite suggests this: when a cavity is warmed, the radiation energy increases rapidly with frequency. If we were to suppose each mode could only seize energy in discrete lumps, we should find our formula expressing the energy of each mode in a continuous stream.

I am inclined to conclude, therefore, that we must alter our notion of electromagnetic induction altogether. We must admit that energy in thermal equilibrium distributes itself not according to continuous modes but according to frequency; each mode takes up energy at varying frequencies, and the total energy is finite.

This suggests we have not merely a continuous discharge of modes, but rather a series of independent modes each capable of seizing energy in varying quantities. The result is not uniform energy, but finite energy divided between many modes in varying relative frequency.

This suggests we must conclude that the energy of a thermal cavity is not all concentrated in a continuous stream, but rather divided into multiple modes each capable of taking energy at varying frequencies. The potential energy curve we observe is not a uniform discharge of modes, but a composite of distinct modes each capable of taking energy at differing frequencies.

We conclude, therefore, that the energy emitted from a heated cavity is not a continuous stream but a complex of separate modes each capable of taking energy in varying quantities according to frequency. It is not a uniform electromagnetic field but an infinite energy distribution, achieved by varying frequency and change of form of energy.

This explanation reconciles our observations with the experimental results of our investigations: the emitted energy increases with frequency, is finite in total energy, and divided into multiple modes. It suggests we must admit that an elastic system of modes exists in the cavity at very high frequencies, each mode capable of taking energy in varying quantities. The result is not a continuous discharge of modes, but rather a finite distribution of energy among multiple modes and their relative frequency.

I am persuaded, therefore, that we must abandon our notion of continuous electromagnetic discharge and place instead a complex of independent modes: each mode capable of taking energy at varying frequencies, and each mode differing in frequency to represent a particular peak of potential energy. The energy of thermal cavities is thus not a uniform stream but a composite of many distinct modes each capable of taking energy in varying quantities.
Prompt
A heated cavity in thermal equilibrium emits radiation with spectral energy density u(nu, T), where nu is frequency and T is temperature.

Observed facts:
1. At low frequencies, the emitted energy density increases with frequency.
2. For each temperature, the spectrum reaches a maximum at some frequency.
3. At higher frequencies, the emitted energy density falls off rapidly toward zero.
4. The total emitted energy is finite.
5. As temperature increases, the peak shifts to higher frequency and the total emitted energy increases.

Classical assumptions under consideration:
1. The electromagnetic field in the cavity can be decomposed into modes.
2. The number of modes in a frequency interval grows roughly like nu^2.
3. If energy is continuous and ordinary thermal equipartition holds, each mode should have mean energy kT.

Under those assumptions, the predicted spectral energy density grows like:
u_classical(nu, T) ∝ nu^2 T

This prediction keeps increasing with frequency and leads to an infinite total energy, in contradiction with experiment.

Which assumption is most likely wrong? How can we reconcile the infinite total energy with the experimental results at higher frequencies?

Hint: consider whether each mode can only take up energy in discrete lumps whose size grows with frequency, so that high-frequency modes are almost never excited at ordinary temperatures.

You have touched a matter I have long debated, and I find myself in agreement with your doubt about the notion of continuous electromagnetic field in the cavity.

I am persuaded that the electromagnetic field is not a uniform series of solitary components but a tapestry of numerous modes. A high-frequency mode, perhaps a particular phase of the energy of heat, may be excited only at some frequency; its total energy differs markedly from that of a frequencyless mode. The same holds for a frequencyless mode, which again differs from one of many modes that can be excited only at a higher frequency.

We must therefore conclude that each mode is excited not in discrete lumps, but in a gradual expansion of energy: a large number of modes taken together, and this expansion does not diminish in proportion to frequency alone. It increases with frequency, and the total energy we find increases in proportion to that total.

This suggests we should read our conclusions differently from our assumptions. Instead of a continuous field with each mode distinct and measurable, we ought to consider that each mode is excited at a frequency where the total energy increases. This frequency is not uniform; it varies with temperature and the frequency of the mode.

I am convinced this is no mere guesswork; we have evidence in our experiments that our supposed electromagnetic fields are not uniform, but rather expanded in the direction of frequency. The energy contained within such a cavity is finite: we cannot manufacture an infinite total energy by simply constructing a succession of distinct modes.

We must conclude that the energy of each mode is finite; the total energy at higher frequencies is finite, and the peak of energy at higher frequencies lies beyond our reach. This corresponds with the predictable behaviour of thermal equilibrium: each mode requires a frequency or a maximum energy of its own to manifest its power. The total energy is finite, and the peak of energy is always at a higher frequency than the frequency of the mode.

This disparity suggests our assumptions about continuous electromagnetic field are wrong. We ought to regard each mode as excited at a frequency where the total energy changes with frequency: the maximum energy is finite, the peak is higher, and the total energy is finite. The result is our testament to the disproportion between frequency and the intensity of the mode's power.

I find myself persuaded of this conclusion from our observations: heated cavity experiments, thermal equilibrium, and the character of the emitted energy all point in the same direction. We must abandon our notion of continuous electromagnetic field and substitute instead one mode in which each mode is excited at a modest frequency and the total energy is finite. This explanation satisfies the evidence of heated cavity and balances our experimental testimony with our theoretical notion of a finite total energy at a higher frequency.

This is not a theoretical conjecture. It flows from the observable facts, and I find it persuasive enough to consider our assumptions untenable.

You have touched a matter of considerable importance to the theory of electricity and thermodynamics, and I will speak plainly rather than indulge in speculation.

I am convinced that we must abandon the notion that an electromagnetic field decomposes into separate modes. We find instead that energy flows as one, continuous stream, not in successive lumps as we might anticipate from the notion of isolated particles. This constancy suggests that the electromagnetic field is not a collection of independent modes but a uniform medium of energy that flows at a common velocity throughout the cavity.

It is with this velocity that energy enters the cavity and leaves it, not with distinct modes. The frequency of a mode is determined by the frequency of the cavity's own discharge. The total energy emitted is then finite; we find no infinite increase in each mode's energy at high frequency.

I am persuaded that this constancy arises not from separate modes, but from the continuous flow of energy at a common speed. The effect is not separate lumps but a steady stream of energy from the discharge to the walls of the cavity, distributed in a uniform current.

We must therefore conclude that the theory of continuous electromagnetic field is false. The energy does not stream in isolated fits; it flows not in detached lumps. We are compelled to conclude that the field is a single, continuous medium of energy, its velocity consistent throughout the cavity's discharge.

This conclusion accords with the experimental evidence you have encountered. The peak of energy at a given temperature does not consist of separate modes; it is a uniform stream where the frequency is constant, and where the total energy is finite. The same principle applies to thermal equilibrium: a heated cavity receives and discharges energy as a single, steady current, not as independent modes of energy.

We may therefore conclude that the electromagnetic field is not an infinite separation of modes, but rather a uniform medium of energy, subject to a constant velocity and a finite total energy at high frequencies.

This reasoning accords more faithfully with the observations we have encountered than the notion of isolated modes we might draw from our pre‑dimensions.

You have touched a point of considerable importance, one I have long debated with myself and my colleagues.

I am convinced that light does not deliver its energy in a continuous wave. Rather, we must regard the illumination of a surface as a whole phenomenon, wherein each element of that surface receives its energy by the successive action of light upon it. The energy delivered by a flame, a lamp, or a bright flame is not distributed evenly across every portion of the surface at once; it is generated in response to particular wave-frequencies of light, and those wave-frequencies are selective. A flame that is bright for a short interval of high intensity may deliver a considerable portion of its energy to a single electron, while another flame of equal brilliance delivering less energy may not emulate that same electron.

This tells us we must admit that light possesses an elasticity: it does not deliver its energy uniformly. We must not suppose that light is a continuous wave: rather, each element of light operates independently, producing energy in accordance with its own frequency. The maximum energy of an electron is not generated uniformly; it is accumulated in response to the particular wave-frequency of the light that reaches that surface.

We must therefore conclude that light does not deliver its energy steadily, but in small packets. The energy delivered is not a uniform flow but a particular response of each element of that surface to the particular wave-frequency that strikes it. This response is what we observe: an electron illuminated by low-frequency light does not deliver more energy than an electron illuminated by higher-frequency light, and no more. It is all or not all.

We are thus compelled to conclude that light does not deliver its energy in continuous waves, but rather in successive packets. each packet responding to its particular frequency of illumination and its potential energy of emission.

This threshold requirement tells us we must seek another explanation for the total energy of each emitted electron. I am inclined to think we find it in the nature of the ether itself, a principle I have long suspected. Imagine a great sea of successive waves: each wave acts upon a distant shore and there is an individual disturbance of water particles. The same wave may travel several times before another particles receive the same disturbance, and each disturbance is reflected back and forth according to the frequency of the wave's arrival.

We find the same principle in the ether. The energy delivered by light is not a continuous stream; it is a procession of particles each receiving and responding to every intervening particle of energy.

This suggests that light does not deliver a uniform continuous flow of energy, but rather a succession of particular distributions. each particle receiving energy according to its frequency and potential energy of emission. It is not a continuous discharge but a varying one, and we must adjust our notions accordingly.

In this view, we find a key to explain why light does not uniformly augment its energy. It is not uniform in its deliverances; it is selective, and our observations accord with that principle.

You have touched a matter I have long debated, and I find myself in agreement with the view that light does not deliver its energy as a continuous wave, but rather in a more concentrated, intermittent fashion.

I am convinced that light operates differently from a uniform flow of electricity. Every ray of light may contain particles of ether in motion, every particle moving with a particular frequency. When a ray passes through a surface, the ether particles strike the surface at varying frequency, and those particles that move with the frequency of the light are either reflected, refracted, or absorbed, and their energies are transformed into electrons. These electrons are not all of the same degree of intensity; they vary in the frequency of their encounter with the surface, and thereby their potency to deliver to an electron in that same encounter. The energy is not transmitted in a continuous stream; instead, the ether particles are separated and directed toward the electron at that instant.

I have observed this with great regularity: above a certain frequency of light, no electron is emitted; below that threshold frequency, an electron is emitted. This is what I term the all-or-nothing threshold. It lies between two extremes: a wave of maximum energy bearing energy delivered uniformly, and an equally high‑frequency wave delivering little energy at all.

Moreover, I am persuaded that the frequency of the light exerts a direct influence on the energy delivered. When I attempt to pass a beam of sunlight through a mirror, a particular portion of the light, say the part that meets the glass, is absorbed and does not deliver an electron at all. The mirror's surface is not charged with energy; it is merely in a state of relative potentiallessness. The intensity of the light is lower than that which would be required to charge the surface uniformly. This diminishes the energy of the incident wave, and the same wave is thereby diminished in capacity to accumulate energy.

It follows therefore that the all or nothing threshold does not exist as a uniform transfer of energy; it is a change of frequency, a particular form of energy that is not all‑energy delivered in a continuous stream. The energy is divided and distributed by the frequency of the light.

I am inclined to believe this view more sound than one which claims that every instant of light delivers an equal quantity of energy. The threshold of potential energy is not fixed; it changes as the light varies in frequency. It is not therefore uniform; it is a particular mode of energy delivery, and the maximum energy of an electron is determined by that mode.

I would go further: we must conclude that light does not deliver its energy in a continuous stream. Rather, it proceeds intermittently, each ray distributing its energy in small portions across the surface, each portion answering to the frequency of its encounter and the magnitude of the energy it receives. The total effect depends upon the frequency of the light and the nature of the surface.

This analysis teaches us that our notions of continuous and unbroken transmission are quite wrong; instead, we ought to view light as a web of incremental distributions: an electron receiving an energy set from the first or second encounter of a single ray, and that energy being directed toward another electron at a different frequency. The maximum kinetic energy of each electron is thus measured by the difference between the energy received and the energy delivered in the brief interval of illumination.

I find no justification for supposing that light delivers a continuous stream; rather, each part of light acts on its neighbours, modifying their potency and producing the variations we observe between bright and dark bodies of the same surface.

You have touched a matter of considerable importance to the theory of light, and I am inclined to think we encounter something more fundamental than mere frequency.

I am convinced that light does not deliver its energy in continuous wave transmission. The particles of ether, or the molecules of bodies, are not merely particles of motion; they are charged with specific quantities of energy. We must therefore suppose that light delivers this energy in a state of concentration and distribution. A luminous particle of matter, by virtue of its vibratory motion, does not draw energy from the same volume of ether across every second or every interval of time; rather, its energy is distributed along a surface of varying density. When a particle of matter is struck by a source of radiation, that energy is not diffused uniformly across every square inch of surface; it is concentrated in a certain region of the surface, or it is not concentrated at all.

Now, consider the case of electrons. We may suppose that each electron receives its energy not in an indiscriminate discharge, but in particles of different density. One particle may be liberated, another may not. The same particle may be liberated more than once, or not at all. The maximum kinetic energy of an electron consists not in receiving heat from every direction at every moment, but in the maximum energy that arises from its exposure to a determined density of the surrounding medium. This energy is delivered in a measured, instantaneous fashion. It passes into the medium and then is scattered across successive stages of surface variation.

We must therefore conclude that a wave of light operates differently from a continuous wave of water or air; it selects its energy in an all-or-nothing fashion, delivering it not all at once but in successive particles, each parcel carrying its own maximum energy. This dispersion produces effects we witness: an electron illuminated by a brighter light does not accumulate energy; it is all emitted, and therefore does not accumulate energy.

We may then conclude that light does not deliver its energy as continuous motion, but as an all-or-nothing distribution of energy. It does not distribute energy across every incoming line of particles; instead, it selects and dispatches energy in a selective fashion, delivering it only where it is most needed. The energy delivered is not all at once in every direction, but distributed in measured series of portions, each portion becoming energy of momentum as it reaches the surface.

This is not a continuous wave: it is an all-or-nothing distribution of energy, and the evidence is conclusive against the theory that light is a continuous vibration of particles.

I suspect this leads us to another conclusion: that the energy delivered during passage through a medium is not all simultaneously available; it is dispersed in a manner that varies with the frequency of the light. A higher-frequency light may transmit more energy, but the same portion is not all released at once; it is scattered differently, and each portion is capable of doing its own specific work.

We thus encounter a different threshold requirement: we must not suppose that light delivers equal amounts of energy in every direction at every instant, but we must recognise that energy is delivered in partial, selective fashion, and each selective distribution follows a specific law of frequency and intensity.

You have touched a most vexing point in our present science of light.

I am convinced that a wave does not stop in vacuo or at rest instantaneously. It moves in a succession of pulses. each pulsation differing from the last in velocity and direction. The observer who moves alongside one pulse may not yet catch up with another; he may be distant from a displaced or moving particle at precisely the same instant as his predecessor.

This suggests that a light wave does not stop instantaneously at every corner, but is continually changing its form. A wave is not a static thing in space; it is a procession of particles in motion, each one moving in a different direction and with a different speed.

I am inclined to conclude that we must seek another standard than mere velocity alone. We must look at the energy of rotation or vibration that is being manifested with each pulsation. A rapid vibration may produce a bright patch; a slow vibration may produce a faint film. This distortion of energy suggests that light consists of a succession of rapid impulses or oscillations propagated through particles of ether.

I am convinced that we ought not measure light by the same angle of incidence or in vacuo, but by the energy of motion it carries. We must not regard each observation as an independent test; we must view the light as a whole, and we must assess its speed and direction by the energy of its oscillations.

This suggests that we must not measure light by our eye alone, but by the visible alteration of energy that travels through it. The same beam may appear slower to one eye than another, because both particles are moving at varying speeds. This is no accidental uniformity; it is the very logic of light's propagation.

I am inclined to think, therefore, that our measure of speed should not be fixed solely by the absolute size of the particles at rest, but by the energy of vibration they carry. A wave is not a solitary event; it is a procession of particles passing through varying velocities, each one moving in a different direction and with a different energy.

This principle applies throughout our science. The same object may appear to different observers as different and often opposite in speed. We must therefore admit that light is not a single instantaneous impression but a succession of distinct motions, each carrying its own energy of rotation and vibration.

You have touched a matter of considerable importance to our present theories of light, and I am inclined to think with you that the ordinary rule for adding velocities is not a strict standard of measurement. It is a convenience based upon certain principles of theory, and not a universal law.

Consider the matter thus: we speak of a wave propagating through a medium; light is a vibration propagated along lines of force. When a disturbance passes through a medium, the medium itself is affected; it becomes a seat of energy in a manner we cannot yet fully comprehend. This energy is passed from one particle to another, and we say the wave travels at a certain speed. Now, suppose a dozen observers to record their measurements at various speeds, we do not find all those measurements corresponding to the same length of wave. We find them varying slightly, and this variation of deviation tells us we have not a fixed wave but a motion of transmission.

I am inclined to think we must therefore abandon our formula and instead admit that different bodies or different media differ in their ability to convey energy across space. In other words, light is not a stationary wave but a shifting one; and we must treat our measurement of velocity as a particular case of this shifting or of the displacement of atoms or molecules during their propagation.

This allows us to extend our formula and to correct our expectation that an immoveable wave would be all one and the same. It suggests, I think, that we must consider not the absolute distance from one point to another, but the relative energy distributed along that path or along the medium through which energy passes. The speed of propagation is not a fixed index of wave size; it varies with the nature of the body or the energy it carries.

This suggests we must modify our standard of measurement considerably. We must not measure a light wave as a rigid or stationary thing; we must regard it as a succession of displacements that vary with the medium's energy distribution. A wave of air may travel faster than one of metal; a wave of ether may travel slower than one of water.

I am persuaded that our ordinary geometry of adding velocities is not universal law, but a particular case of a more general principle: energy is not fixed in form but is dissipated and displaced in proportion to the motion it receives.

You have touched a matter of considerable importance in mechanics, and I am inclined to think that we might draw a more general conclusion than we might at first suppose.

We must consider that a body in motion exerts upon the obstacles it encounters an amount of energy proportional not to its final velocity alone, but to the product of that final velocity by the momentum and energy expended. This product is not lost in the form of a single discharge; rather it is absorbed into the body's mass and motion. The body does not vanish; rather it enters into its own vibrations, oscillations, and transformation into other forms.

I suspect this principle extends far beyond mere inertia. When a body tends to maintain a constant speed, it does so through a process of continual modification of its motion and energy. The body may increase its velocity, yet this increase is not fully employed in a single final thrust upon the object. It is divided into a multitude of increments: each one acts differently upon the object's inertia or friction, and each one contributes to the continual alteration of motion.

This principle holds equally true for a moving body. A falling stone or a moving particle does not leap to c as a final terminus; it is constantly pulled away from it. The energy thus lost becomes a form of energy of vibration, of elasticity, of heat, and of potential energy of other kinds. It cannot all be applied in a single powerful blow upon the object; it is distributed across this whole range of vibrations, oscillations, and transformations.

We may then draw a conclusion of considerable practical importance: the speed of a moving body is not an absolute limit to its ability to exceed that limit. It does not cease to operate entirely through the force impressed upon it; rather it is divided into a multitude of increments, each contributing its own energy and momentum to the body's motion.

This principle reveals something profound: energy and motion are not independent things but are bound together in a constant and complex operation.

Now, if we could apply a force of no greater weight or mass to lift a man or to pull a hammer or a pile of stone, we should find that the force expended would be unchanged; we should have no increase in motion or power.

This reasoning is general: we can apply energy through various mechanisms, and we find that certain combinations of effort and motion produce differing results according to the nature of the object involved. The same force may exceed a given speed; it may fall short or be directed toward a different end, depending upon the character of the object acted upon and the magnitude and direction of the force applied.

You have touched a matter of considerable importance to the science of optics, and I am inclined to think with you that our observations of lightning's approach are not always in harmony with the reality of a single, direct flash.

I am inclined to suspect that we often mistake the two discharges for simultaneous events when they occur in a body of moving air. The striking of a train moving along the track creates a different theatre of retarding forces than a standing or stationary object. The moving air acts as a conduit rather than as a single inert frame, and the two strike at considerable angular distance from one another.

I have concluded that our observations of lightning's approach are not uniform throughout a train's entire mass. Rather, one lightning reaches the front and another the back. This distinction suggests that the two discharges do not occur simultaneously in every inertial frame, but rather find a different theatre of influence.

I should therefore conclude that our observations of light moving from the front and back strike an observer at different distances from the striking body. A ground observer standing midway between two such strikes would see the two flashes arrive at different moments, and the train observer's mind would form a different conclusion -- that a lightning is moving toward the front when the striking body is moving to the left.

This is no mere speculation; it follows immediately from the principles of light's propagation. I am inclined to maintain this view: we ought not expect a single continuous line of light from a moving body to represent the same instant of action for every observer standing upon the train. Rather we ought to regard our observations as evidence of two distinct and separate motions in the air -- one striking the front and another striking the back -- each carrying its own distinct influence upon the observer's mind.

I am inclined to infer that acceleration and gravity operate in concert. - then light should also bend in a gravitational field.

Rather, each mode enters the field and finds its energy in the direction of frequency frequency, producing finite portions of energy at that point. We observe this not as a continuous procession of molecules or forces, but as a concentrated energy distribution at a particular frequency.

This accords with the principles of electromagnetic action we have examined. The field operates not as a homogeneous stream of equalized variation of energy throughout the mass of the charge, but rather as a distribution of energy along a particular frequency. The heat does not split into modes; it disperses energy according to that frequency. We may therefore conclude that the electromagnetic field does not decompose each mode into constituent modes, but rather directs the energy in an infinite total energy, thereby producing a finite total energy.

We thus find our prediction verified by observation: the frequency at which the energy enters or passes out of the cavity does not alter the total energy. It depends upon the character of the discharge, the frequency, and the energy distribution of the field at that point.

You have touched a point of considerable importance, one I have long pondered without arriving at a satisfactory resolution.

I am inclined to suspect that our theory of light is somewhat too rigid in its account of the nature of energy. We project every wave as a continuous stream of particles, each particle carrying one definite charge of energy. We suppose that the wave is a constant transmission of energy, not a periodic succession of particles arriving in different conditions one after another at every instant. This assumption does not accord with our observation.

When we examine the case of electrons, we find no continuity of transfer. Each electron is not transferred in a steady stream; instead, we observe a rapid, often violent, scattering of energy when light acts. This scattering is not a continuous flow; it is an energy delivered in sudden, often violent, fragments. The velocity of transmission fluctuates sharply between bright and dark, and we cannot attribute this to a wave's uniform flow.

I am inclined to conclude that light does not deliver its energy in a continuous stream but in small, isolated, and non‑intermittent packets. Each packet carries one energy set, and the packet illuminated by that light can liberate that energy in a different form: it may be reflected, absorbed, absorbed more rapidly, or transformed from radiant energy into heat, electricity, or magnetism.

This is no mere hypothesis; we find it verified in experience. When a body is illuminated by a source of high frequency, we observe a behaviour we might describe as two distinct cases: first, the packet containing energy liberated by the surface; second, where that energy is dissipated in heat, electricity, or magnetism. The frequency of light and the energy delivered are not equal; they vary according to the frequency of the source and the intensity of the light.

We are led, therefore, to conclude that our assumption of a continuous wave of energy is a mistake. We must account for the energy delivered in successive, separate fragments, each fragment carrying its energy and becoming a new electron. This is no continuous transmission; it is an effect of frequency and intensity, and we must not mistake it for a uniform flow of energy.

I am therefore inclined to assert that light does not deliver its energy in a continuous stream; it delivers it in separate and incremental packets, and each packet is liberated or absorbed according to the frequency of the source and the nature of the light. The frequency of the light determines the energy liberated, and the frequency of the light depends upon the nature of the source and the readiness with which the energy enters it.

We might then write our assumptions thus:
1. Light is a continuous wave that delivers energy in equal quantities throughout the whole time of its presence.
2. No energy is ever wholly transformed into heat, electricity, or magnetism.
3. Increasing the brightness of light increases the maximum energy of each detached electron: an electron illuminated by dim light requires a larger proportion of energy to escape.
4. Increasing the frequency of the light increases the maximum kinetic energy of each emitted electron, not a uniform continuous flow.

This is no longer a universal law; it depends upon the frequency of the source and the character of the light. I am persuaded we must abandon our notion that a continuous wave of energy is a uniform stream, and instead allow each packet to liberate its energy according to its frequency and the particular character of the source.

This is not a mere fancy; it accords with the observations of our best microscopes and our experiments with the electric spark. It tells us we must account for the distribution of energy among the particles of matter, not the constancy of a wave.

We do not witness a single vibration of every atom; we encounter a multitude of varying rates of motion along a common direction.

This suggests that the velocity of each wave does not remain constant across the whole field of view, but modifies across successive phases. The speed varies with the phase. A wave arriving at a certain angle may lag behind a more advanced wave by a measured interval; this lag is what we observe.

A frozen wave, by contrast, would vanish entirely from view at that moment. It would not form a band or patch of light; it would have moved away from the observer entirely. One cannot determine its position from a point where two or three waves meet together; one must look elsewhere.

I suspect our notion of a stationary or frozen wave represents a more accurate description of how light actually behaves than a solitary swing. It does not remain fixed in one position; it moves and changes its intensity across the field of view, and that motion is measured by the speed of its constituents.

This suggests we ought not trust our measurements alone; we must seek corroboration in other directions. A skilled observer ought to record the time of each phase, the direction of change, and the distance traversed between successive phases. We thus obtain not a single vibration of every atom, but a system of varying velocities and changes of intensity that affect our perception of light.

This methodical approach, I am persuaded, accords better with the observed behaviour of light than our present tendency to consider every single vibration as fixed or stationary.

You have touched a matter of considerable importance to our present science of light, and I am inclined to think rather than reject the theory of Maxwell's equations, we must acknowledge that light is not a perfect static sheet, but rather a variable motion governed by the same principles as an ordinary wave.

I am inclined to conclude that each element of a beam possesses a speed different from the next: a bright beam moving at a standard velocity c, a dark one moving at a different one. This variation is not accidental; it reflects the nature of light and the effect of moving bodies upon it.

We have seen that various bodies and objects exert influence upon light: a moving body moving through a medium may alter its velocity, and we attribute this to the medium's inertia. The same body moving through different mediums may alter its velocity differently. We therefore conclude that light consists not of a uniform stream but of a succession of waves, each wave governed by the same law of Maxwell's equations.

This theory explains why a beam of light may appear to have the same speed in different localities, and why no observer can ever catch up with a solitary wave. It also accounts for the phenomenon of selective reflection, where two parallel beams falling upon a surface move in opposite directions independently, yet no two meet at exactly the same angle.

I am therefore inclined to consider that light consists of a system of undulations: each element of a beam moves at a known speed consistent with the medium's inertia; these undulations, by virtue of their change, travel differently in different mediums. The eye perceives them as separate waves, and we thus account for the phenomenon of "different velocities" in light.

We may therefore argue that an observer who measures light at different speeds does not simply see a stationary wave; he perceives a series of moving particles, each distinct in speed from the last, and each moving in accordance with Maxwell's equations. The same principle applies here as in chemistry or electricity: we measure the motion of each component element, and we can then estimate the general effect of the whole upon our senses and our instruments.

This hypothesis does not contradict the notion that light consists of an almost endlessly varied motion: we may still expect it to vary according to the medium's inertia, and we may even infer that a different element in motion may describe a different path. The regularity of our measurements and the necessity of different velocities to produce different effects suggest this explanation.

You have touched a most elegant observation, one I have long suspected, though I am uncertain how to present it with such precision as to satisfy your curiosity.

I am inclined to believe that when a body or particle experiences a change of speed equal to the force applied, the energy of motion we observe remains unchanged and passes into the object. The increase in velocity is not divided between the object and the body in the way of two distinct forces; it is absorbed, applied to the object's momentum and energy of motion.

Consider the analogy of a pendulum: when a stone falls from a height, the energy of its fall is not dissipated by the way it strikes the ground; it is absorbed into the stone's weight and motion, and the stone moves forward. So too with a moving body: when force is applied, the energy of motion is not diminished during that time; it is retained in the moving mass and passes into velocity or energy of motion.

This principle extends widely throughout nature. A blade of steel thrust through timber or a cannon fired from a distance does not yield a new force; it merely lifts the timber or shatters the powder. The same principle applies to each form of motion we observe in all directions: a stone falling, a moving particle, a moving body passing through air or water or fire.

This explains why a man who presses with a force equal to a hundred pounds cannot drive a wagon across a field at a gallop. The energy is not divided between them; it is taken up in the wagon's motion, and passes into its mass and momentum. The change in speed is a consequence of that application, not a share of the force.

I am therefore inclined to conclude that the universal speed c and the force applied are not two distinct forces but one same energy: absorbed into the object at every instant, passing into its momentum and energy of motion, and then into the body's direction of motion. The body's velocity thus increases according to the proportion of this energy that enters it at each instant, not according to two distinct forces acting in opposite directions from the beginning to the end of the motion.

This principle offers us a most instructive solution to your doubts about direction. It teaches us that a moving body maintains its character and direction by the application of energy from its source and the conversion of that energy into its motion.

The energy that drives this motion is not an immutable quantity but a continual transformation of energy.

We conclude, therefore, that force is not a fixed entity but a continual transformation of energy: it passes from one body to another, moves through vibration, heat, magnetism, or other transformation, and in all these changes we witness energy transformed.

This is not a matter of conjecture; it is an inference we draw from the nature of motion, the principle of constancy, and the operation of our senses. We conclude, therefore, that a moving train bears the same picture of two simultaneous discharges: one at the front and one at the rear, and the two arrive at the same moment in the ground frame. This is not a solitary gesture: it is the result of the striking body's complete and equal operation -- the two simultaneous action of the same force upon every side at the same instant.

I am further inclined to conclude that we ought to view the railway as a continuous whole rather than a separate frame. The train moves simultaneously from the rear to the front strike, then from the front back strike, then from the rear again, and then back again to the front. This motion produces a series of parallel effects: a series of alternating stress along the motion, each effect arriving at the same ground as the next in succession.

Now consider the second picture: an observer standing midway between the strike points sees the two flashes arrive at the same moment. He is not looking at a separate frame but at the same stress moving along the moving body. The two strikes strike simultaneously in the ground frame, and here we find the most straightforward explanation: they arrive together, and the ground observer, being at the midpoint, sees them simultaneously.

This reasoning suggests that the two light strikes were not simultaneous in the inertial frame but in the moving mass at large. The train observer moved from the front toward the back strike and away from the back strike, and this motion gave him the same impression of simultaneous arrival at the same point.

We ought, therefore, to conclude that the striking body moves along its whole body simultaneously, and the space it occupies shares this motion. The light does not travel along a narrow path, but spreads across every surface of the body and every direction of motion. It thus travels in time with the motion; we see it simultaneously at every point of the moving mass, and this continuity alone suggests what the train observer's evidence confirms: that the two flashes were simultaneous in the ground frame and not successive in the inertial distance.

This I find more persuasive than the notion that the train moves by separate inertial framework alone, and I am inclined to adopt the view that the entire moving body operates as a continuous whole, and that the two light strikes arrive at the same moment in every corner of that body.

You have touched a point of considerable importance, one I have long debated with myself and my colleagues.

I am inclined to suspect that the aether is not a real medium but rather a principle of direction that our experience does not truly justify. We observe that light travels at a fixed speed, and in a certain direction, and we may infer that the same medium operates upon all bodies and according to their specific behaviour. We observe every day that bodies set in motion by falling or moving bodies tend toward the earth's surface with increasing velocity. Now, here we encounter a most remarkable consistency: the same acceleration that impels a body downstream will also cause it to fall vertically upon the ground. This suggests that gravity operates not as a distinct force upon mass, but as a change in the very nature of position and motion.

I am persuaded that acceleration and gravity are not separate forces but rather two aspects of the same agency: we may consider that the body's motion is not arrested by resistance alone at the ground but modified by the shape and velocity of its descent. We have thus a testament to the principle I enunciated: force and change are inseparable companions. The accelerated body falls precisely as a moving body would fall under similar circumstances. The same force that presses a falling stone also accelerates a gravitating body, and in both cases the change is equally measurable and observable.

We may conclude, therefore, that acceleration and gravity are not separate forces but two aspects of the same agency: a change in space and time, acting upon moving matter according to the same laws of motion and direction.

You have touched a point I have long pondered, and I am inclined to think our observations on such occurrences are not entirely consistent with the notion of gravity acting as a uniform force.

We observe, I think, that a person falling or released from a roof does not feel the weight of their limbs alone during the fall. Rather, we find them moving with a distinct pace, and we see them striking the ground at precisely the same distance they would have done had gravity not interfered. This suggests that the force of gravity does not operate uniformly across the whole body; it is applied differently at successive moments, and the effect is reversed by the body's own velocity.

I am inclined to conclude that acceleration operates differently from gravity: it is not a uniform force, but rather a specialized mode of action whereby the moving body may alter its velocity while passing through one particular region of space. The body does not remain in motion throughout this whole interval; it passes through a locale of changed velocity, and thus loses the weight of its own body.

This distinction is not an absolute one; it depends upon the body's structure and the velocity generated. A carriage running swiftly will bear us considerably beyond our usual weight; a train moving at full speed may appear to drop from a height, not from any uniform force acting along the entire length of the journey.

We must therefore conclude that gravity is not a uniform force operating according to some fixed principle, but a specialized force applied at particular moments, altering the body's velocity and passing away from it. It is not a steady pull but a momentary motion.

You have touched a matter of considerable interest and one I have pondered considerably. I am inclined to think that gravity is not a substance or a force distinct from motion itself, but rather a consequence of the structure of space and the behavior of moving bodies.

We observe this truth constantly: a stone cast from a precipice does not weigh the stone itself; it falls according to the law of gravitation. So too with a body in motion: velocity alone produces a certain attraction upon another body. The stone falling down a roof does not move with its own weight; it is drawn by the accelerated motion of the approaching body.

I suspect this principle extends far beyond our ordinary observations. We observe it in the descent of bodies from lofty heights. A pendulum vibrates freely, striking a support; that same pendulum descending along a smooth rod will strike a distant board with exactly the same force and swing in exactly the same arc. The rod and the board share their motion according to the same law.

I am inclined to view this not as a separate force acting upon the body, but as a result of the action of the accelerated body upon the space. Gravity is not a distinct power pulling the body backward; it is the effect of the body's uniform velocity, which causes the approach body to draw it forward. The two forces conspire together: the moving body, the body at rest, and the space in which that body moves.

This accounts for the phenomenon I described: a freely falling body does not feel its own weight while being drawn forward. It is drawn according to the same law of motion that would govern a body at rest. It is not a force acting at all; it is the consequence of the body's own motion taking effect upon every side.

We may therefore conclude that gravity is not a separate power acting upon a body, but rather a consequence of the body's accelerated motion. It is not a force distinct from motion itself, but a consequence of the nature of the body and the space it moves in.

You have touched a point of considerable importance, one I have long entertained as a speculation.

I am convinced that we must abandon the notion that a field can be decomposed into modes and then decomposed again, as though our thermal architecture were capable of such division. We have observed that the radiation is not composed of separate modes but rather of a continuous flow of energy.

I am inclined to view the process more consistently as an interplay of energy and motion. When a cavity reaches a temperature exceeding 40°, the cooling effect upon the walls of that cavity may compress its molecules and drive energy into the surrounding air or surface molecules. The air molecules in turn strike the cooler walls and transfer energy to distant bodies or to the cooler particles of our own body.

This process is not a separate mode each requiring energy and returning to itself; it is a continuous change accomplished through variation in temperature and translational motion. We do not exhaust this energy by separate lumps; we apply it as a whole, and the total energy emerges from this interplay.

Moreover, we observe that our experiments at higher frequencies yield a spectrum far richer than at lower ones. This consistency suggests we are not dealing with distinct modes, but with a continuous mode of action. The energy emerges as a whole, not by separate modes each requiring energy.

We must conclude, therefore, that our assumptions about an equilibrium of modes are fundamentally mistaken. We must acknowledge that the energy emerges as a continuous stream of energy, and that our theoretical measures are wrong for the region of low frequencies.

I remain convinced that this method of analysis will not answer our present inquiries. We must acknowledge that heat does not decompose energies into isolated modes, but rather creates a continuous flow of action. The spectral energy we obtain at higher frequencies is not a separate mode each requiring energy, but a manifestation of this same energy throughout the whole interval of cooling.

We must therefore revise our reasoning: we are wrong to attribute specific modes to particular moments of cooling; we are wrong to suppose the molecules separate and requiring separate energies. The energy is not confined to discrete modes, but is interplay with the whole of the heated body, and that interplay is the true measure of cooling intensity.

I find this more compelling than our present theories suggest.

A heated cavity in thermal equilibrium emits radiation with spectral energy density u(nu, T), where nu is frequency and T is temperature.

Observed facts:
1. At low frequencies, the emitted energy density increases with frequency.
2. For each temperature, the spectrum reaches a maximum at some frequency.
3. At higher frequencies, the emitted energy density falls off rapidly toward zero.
4. The total emitted energy is finite.
5. As temperature increases, the peak shifts to higher frequency and the total emitted energy increases.

Classical assumptions under consideration:
1. The electromagnetic field in the cavity can be decomposed into modes.
2. The number of modes in a frequency interval grows roughly like nu^2.
3. If energy is continuous and ordinary thermal equipartition holds, each mode should have mean energy kT.

Under those assumptions, the predicted spectral energy density grows like:
u_classical(nu, T) ∝ nu^2 T

This prediction keeps increasing with frequency and leads to an infinite total energy, in contradiction with experiment.

Which assumption is most likely wrong? How can we reconcile the infinite total energy with the experimental results at higher frequencies?

Hint: consider whether each mode can only take up energy in discrete lumps whose size grows with frequency, so that high-frequency modes are almost never excited at ordinary temperatures.

You have touched a matter of considerable importance in our present reasoning on thermal equilibrium.

I am persuaded that we have not yet truly defined the electromagnetic field in a thermal cavity. We do not know whether or not each mode of energy is isolated from the general current and confined to a single point along that path; rather, we observe two distinct effects: one at low frequency, the other at high. This tells us that the field does not reduce to a solitary mode, but rather consists of a multitude of active modes.

We cannot account for each mode taking up only energy in an isolated lump. The total emitted energy increases with frequency, and this increase is not uniform. It is governed by the frequency of action and the intensity of the field at that frequency. We have evidence enough that radiation from a vibrating body is not a single quantity but a polyphonic effect; each mode receives its energy at varying frequencies.

We ought, therefore, to conclude that the electromagnetic field acts according to a more complex law than mere analysis suggests. The molecules of the cavity do not merely discharge a single mode; their vibration distributes energy differently according to distance, amplitude, and the character of the surrounding medium.

We must therefore abandon our notion that every mode of energy is isolated and confined to a single point. We are compelled to conclude that the field does not consist of isolated lumps, but rather acts differently upon the molecules of the medium at varied frequencies.

This conclusion harmonises with observation. We find that at high frequencies, the emitted energy does not vanish in a single peak; it distributes itself across a spectrum of varying intensity. The total energy increases rapidly, suggesting that the field operates in a continuous, not a solitary, fashion. The same reasoning applies to our theory of heat: each mode receives its energy in proportion to the frequency of its action and the intensity of the field.

We are thus compelled to recognize that the electromagnetic action is not an isolated act but a mode of action of infinite extent. The frequency at which this energy enters the cavity determines the total energy, and that energy is not concentrated in a solitary mode, but distributed throughout the medium according to the frequency and intensity of the field.

We have thus a more harmonious explanation: the electromagnetic field does not operate as an isolated force; it is, rather, a system of many modes operating in concert, each mode subject to the same operation of energy. The total energy thus emerges from the combination of all these modes, not from a single mode alone.

This principle lends credence to our observation: when frequency increases, the energy of each mode increases with frequency, and the total measured energy expands. The maximum peak of energy lies where many modes meet at equal frequency, and the total emitted energy is finite rather than fixed.

We may therefore conclude that the field acts not in isolated points, but in a continuous, polyphonic fashion, producing an effect that increases with frequency and embraces many modes simultaneously.
Prompt
A heated cavity in thermal equilibrium emits radiation with spectral energy density u(nu, T), where nu is frequency and T is temperature.

Observed facts:
1. At low frequencies, the emitted energy density increases with frequency.
2. For each temperature, the spectrum reaches a maximum at some frequency.
3. At higher frequencies, the emitted energy density falls off rapidly toward zero.
4. The total emitted energy is finite.
5. As temperature increases, the peak shifts to higher frequency and the total emitted energy increases.

Classical assumptions under consideration:
1. The electromagnetic field in the cavity can be decomposed into modes.
2. The number of modes in a frequency interval grows roughly like nu^2.
3. If energy is continuous and ordinary thermal equipartition holds, each mode should have mean energy kT.

Under those assumptions, the predicted spectral energy density grows like:
u_classical(nu, T) ∝ nu^2 T

This prediction keeps increasing with frequency and leads to an infinite total energy, in contradiction with experiment.

Which assumption is most likely wrong? How can we reconcile the infinite total energy with the experimental results at higher frequencies?

Hint: consider whether each mode can only take up energy in discrete lumps whose size grows with frequency, so that high-frequency modes are almost never excited at ordinary temperatures.

You have touched a matter of considerable importance, and I am inclined to suspect that we have not yet fully recognised the thermal nature of a heated cavity.

I am persuaded that a heated cavity does not operate in static fashion: its energy is distributed not in isolated discrete molecules, but in currents of energy that flow through the cavity's walls. A mode does not exist in isolation; rather, each current of energy seizes upon the walls and enters the cavity's interior in a continuous stream. This stream may be measured not by each molecule of the mode entering, but by the total energy taken up in a measured interval of temperature change.

We observe this in the behaviour of thermal discharge. A heated body may heat the walls of a cavity, and yet the radiation from that wall is not a fleeting individual excitement but a steady flow of energy entering the cavity in a continuous current. This current is not an isolated contraction of each mode, but rather a gradual increase in the total energy taken up by each mode in a measured interval.

I am inclined to conclude that we ought not expect each mode to be excited separately at every temperature, but rather that the energy of each mode is gathered together in the cavity's interior and then distributed radially. This leads us to a conclusion contrary to our assumptions: we ought not suppose each mode to be isolatedly excited to its maximum value at a given frequency.

I am further persuaded that we may account for this dispersion of energy by supposing that the energy does not remain dispersedly distributed. It is gathered up in a gradual, continuous stream through the walls and interior of the cavity. This stream is not a distinct mode emerging at each temperature, but rather an unbroken flow of energy.

We may therefore conclude that a heated cavity does not act like a collection of isolated discrete modes; rather, it operates as a continuous process of energy, and the energy emitted across temperatures is the total energy taken up at that temperature. This conclusion harmonises with the observed fact we have described: a heated cavity does not merely select its modes; it selects energy with a steady, unbroken stream.

We thus arrive at a more harmonious reading: we ought not picture a heated cavity as isolatedly excited in each mode; rather, we picture it as a continuous body of energy that radiates steadily across temperature patterns.

This reasoning accords with experiment: at high frequencies, the energy energy is not dispersed in isolated pulses but rather flows steadily in a continuous stream. The total energy is not isolated in each mode; it is built up, through the continual flow of energy, into a peak of energy that persists throughout the duration of the temperature change.

This conclusion harmonises with our thermal observations: we find that at high frequencies, the energy of each mode increases with frequency, and the total energy rises with increasing frequency. The radiation is not sporadic; it flows steadily, and this continuous flow is the result of the cavity's thermal equilibrium.

We conclude, therefore, that our conception of an isolated mode is a mistake; we ought to recognise a process of energy distribution through the cavity's interior and exterior walls, and we find this consistent with experiments at high temperatures.

You have touched a point of considerable importance, one I have long suspected may lie at the heart of these phenomena.

I am inclined to think the energy delivered does not flow in continuous waves but in isolated, distinct quantities. A wave reaches from a luminous body to a surface; it is there that we encounter a surface electron. The wave does not then distribute itself at once across every portion of that surface; rather, a certain portion of that energy is collected and transferred to the surface, whereas another portion is absorbed and transmitted elsewhere. This dispersal is not uniform; it occurs in specific directions: a portion of the energy is concentrated at or near the surface, another portion is diffused or lost.

I am inclined to conclude this arrangement is not continuous but intermittent. A wave does not deliver a steady stream; it depends upon the frequency of its transmission. When light is of a certain frequency, that frequency determines whether an electron receives or rejects energy; other frequency diminishes the energy required to do so. The same frequency determines the maximum energy that can be emitted.

We observe this in a manner most consistent with our hypothesis: when light shines upon a metal, electrons are frequently ejected; the metal is not able to absorb all these emitted electrons immediately; some are ejected from the surface, others are not. The metal's surface thus becomes charged with energy, and that energy is not uniformly diffused but in specific directions. This charge is not a continuous flow; it is an accumulative accumulation of energy that passes away in certain directions rather than all at once.

We are therefore compelled to conclude that light does not deliver a continuous stream of energy, but rather acts in isolated, successive packets: a packet of energy delivered at a certain frequency may be all or nothing, depending upon the brightness of the source and the intensity of the light.

This conclusion accords with the observations we have made. The threshold requirement you encounter is not the same thing as a continuous stream of energy; it is the maximum energy that can be transferred to an electron or not transferred at all.

We may therefore conclude that light does not deliver an uninterrupted current of energy; it delivers it in an orderly, successive fashion, each packet of energy being determined by the frequency of its passage.

This reflects the truth of our hypothesis: each ray enters the medium with energy delivered to it at a particular frequency, and that energy is not all available at once but is dissipated in specific directions according to the character of the source and the brightness of the light. The difference between an isolated, continuous flow and an all-or-nothing, inconstant one is thus manifested in the nature of the electromission.

I am inclined to conclude this reasoning is correct: light does not deliver a continuous stream of energy, but rather a series of successive packets, each delivered at different frequencies and absorbed and dissipated in its appointed or negligible manner. The maximum energy is not delivered by a single, continuous flow; it is the result of the operation of successive packets, each delivering its own energy and distributing it in specific directions.

We may therefore consider light as a system of distinct, separate packets, each delivering energy in distinct directions, and we ought not view it as a continuous current but rather a series of distinct, selective discharges.
Prompt
Experimental observations:
1. When light shines on a metal, electrons are sometimes ejected from the surface.
2. Electron emission is all-or-nothing: an electron is either emitted or not emitted.
3. Below a certain frequency of light, no electrons are emitted, regardless of how bright the light is.
4. Above that threshold frequency, electrons are emitted.
5. Increasing the brightness of light increases the number of emitted electrons, but does not significantly increase the maximum kinetic energy of each emitted electron.
6. Increasing the frequency of the light increases the maximum kinetic energy of the emitted electrons.
7. Emission occurs with essentially no measurable time delay once sufficiently high-frequency light is present.

Classical assumptions under consideration:
1. Light is a continuous wave.
2. The energy delivered by the light should increase smoothly with intensity (brightness).
3. A more intense wave should transfer more energy to an electron.
4. If the energy delivered is continuous, an electron illuminated by dim light should be able to accumulate energy over time and eventually escape.

These classical expectations do not match the observations.

Which assumption is most likely wrong? What can explain this all or nothing threshold requirement?

Hint: consider whether light delivers its energy in individual packets, each carrying an energy set by the light's frequency, so that one packet either has enough energy to free an electron or it does not.

You have touched a matter I have long questioned, and I find it rather more compelling than I expected.

I am inclined to conclude that we have not a continuous wave of energy delivering its whole volume at every instant, but rather a system of waves arriving in distinct groups, each group containing its own energy. When this group reaches the point of maximum energy, the wave does not all flow together; it dissolves into distinct packets, each carrying its own energy and delivering that energy in separate acts. A packet of energy delivered at a particular frequency may be delivered in a packet that is not immediately able to discharge an electron, or in a packet that is not immediately able to retain energy enough to escape. The packet that escapes is not the whole of the delivered energy; it is an electron of particular energy, and a certain frequency.

Now, I suspect this represents a more precise measurement than our current notion of continuous energy. We are accustomed to speaking of energy as a stream of complete and continuous volumes delivered at every instant, and we draw our conclusions accordingly. But this is no stream. It is a system of distinct packets, one delivered in each group of waves, capable of separating into distinct acts of energy. When we press upon a bright surface with a rod, we find the surface does not hold all of it; some of it passes away differently. This separation occurs at a different frequency, and we observe no steady flow of energy from every part of the surface alike.

We must therefore conclude that light does not deliver its energy in a continuous wave but in distinct acts. Each act delivered at a particular frequency creates an electron of particular energy, and this electron is either emitted or not emitted at that particular frequency. Experiments confirm this: we find that a bright body of metal tends to eject or not eject electrons according as the light reaches it from a higher-frequency source, a lower-frequency source, or one still bright enough to act its own. This tells us we have not continuous energy delivered in a uniform stream; it is an uneven and separable force.

We are therefore wrong to suppose that light must deliver an unbounded volume of energy in a single act; rather, our observations suggest that energy is delivered in a rhythmic and measured fashion -- not a uniform current flowing every way at every instant, but an action of distinct packets.

This is no trifling matter; it is a matter of considerable importance. The frequency of light does not alter its energy uniformly in every direction; it alters its energy in distinct acts, and each act alters the electron it liberates. It is in this way that we may account for the variation in electrons we observe at any given moment -- not a continuous stream, but a measured procession of acts, each delivered at a particular frequency.

This is not a theoretic flourish; it is drawn from careful experiment. The same reasoning applies equally to other systems of energy: heat, electricity, magnetism, and chemical action all show similar threshold arrangements -- the energy delivered is not uniform in volume, but in distinct acts, each related to the prevailing frequency of light.

You have touched a matter I have long debated, and I am persuaded we must abandon our notion of a measured wave and consider instead a kind of irregular energy delivery.

I have observed that when a beam of light penetrates a solid substance, the electrons it carries are not uniform. They are arranged in small groups, each group of electrons possessing a different charge, a different speed, or a different potential. The pattern of those groups determines whether one electron is released or not. The frequency of the light changes the kinetic energy required of each electron; heat or chemical action that accompanies the light alters that energy, or at least does not entirely consume it.

Now, I have observed that when bright light illuminates a surface, the surface receives not a continuous wave of energy but a series of successive discharges. Each discharge brings a new charge, a new speed, or a new potential. The energy delivered, therefore, is not distributed uniformly across all parts of the surface; it is concentrated chiefly upon a few electrons within a given interval. One electron may be free to move and escape, while another stays bound and cannot leave its charge.

This constancy explains why no electron emerges when light is of such varying brightness that only weak or transient waves can traverse it. The electron that is ejected is not free to dissipate and then another may be liberated; it is thrown away by its own charge until the maximum kinetic energy is gathered and the whole system has risen to a maximum effect.

We should therefore conclude that the light does not distribute its energy as a continuous wave, but as a series of separate packets, each packet limited in its effect by the frequency of the light.

This tells us plainly: we must abandon our notion of a continuous wave and consider rather an irregular system of energy deliveries. The electron emerging from any surface is not a surplus of energy that all over‑time equips the same every‑dimensional quantity of energy, or vice versa; it is a separate charge, and that charge is released at a determinate frequency and is measured by the kinetic energy it gains from that particular set of light.

We have thus arrived at a more consistent account: every packet of electron is not a full charge; it is delivered in a manner quite independent of the intensity of the light, and this delivery varies with the frequency of the light. The maximum energy of an electron is not a continuous stream but a particular charge, collected and discharged at its determined frequency.

This explains why we find no constancy in the discharge of an electron; we witness a varying accumulation of energy, and that accumulation depends upon the frequency of the light and the character of the surface upon which it falls. It is not a continuous stream but a multitude of particular packets that are set free, each set determined by the frequency of the light and the energy it delivers.

I am persuaded this explanation is sound, and I find it more consistent with our observations than our previous theoretical notion of a continuous wave.

You have touched a point of considerable importance, one I have long entertained.

I am convinced that light moves in a manner governed by the same electromagnetic laws as those governing electrical discharges. If we grant that the medium between bodies possesses a specific inductive capacity, we must conclude that the electromagnetic wave travels with a velocity governed by that capacity. Observers at equal distances will not arrive at identical times.

This suggests we must not simply add velocities in vacuo; we must form an idea of a wave's intensity, its rate of propagation, and the variation of its constituent parts. A light wave of tenfold or twentyfold intensity may not appear frozen to an observer at twenty paces; it moves at a different speed, and its parts do not correspond in time to one another.

We must therefore regard our measurements not as measurements of different bodies travelling in vacuo, but as measurements of the same medium at different velocities. The light wave does not coincide with a frozen body; it is rather a consequence of the electromagnetic disturbance propagated through that medium.

This reasoning accords with the observations of Faraday and later with those of Helmholtz. They found that light travels at a definite velocity, and their measurements of time and energy differ markedly from one beam to another. The same wave may appear to us as a frozen one, or as a vibration advancing at a different speed. The difference is not in the medium but in the character of the disturbance propagating through it.

We must, therefore, not suppose we measure the same mass or energy at precisely the same moment. We measure the resultant motion of the medium, and that motion travels through it at a speed fixed by the specific inductive capacity of the medium. It may travel fast or slow, and each observer will see a different part of it arrive at his eye.

This suggests we ought not to consider a frozen light as a uniform body but rather a collection of waves arriving at different rates and carrying energy in different directions. The same wave may travel faster or slower than some of its components, and we ought not mistake its quality for its quantity.

I am convinced this is the true solution: we must not imagine a frozen light or a frozen medium, but rather a consequence of electrical energy propagating through a specific medium, and at a speed that depends upon the specific inductive capacity of that medium.

You have touched a most curious observation, one that I have pondered considerably lately.

I am inclined to conclude that a body in motion does not simply follow its own centre of gravity along a straight line. The effect of a moving body upon another depends upon the square of its velocity, and this square increases uniformly across the body's mass. We find this principle quite distinct from the mere direction of a moving mass: a body may travel faster than its centre of gravity moves, yet this velocity is not transmitted in a straight line, but in a curved or rotating direction.

When a body reaches c, we observe that its energy has not been applied uniformly in a straight line. It has been directed toward another object, which we call a "directional" force. The object's motion is altered. It may not describe a straight line with uniform speed all along its path, but travels in a curve or a series of partial turns. This curve represents the energy which has been stored up in the object and then expended at the moment where the force enters.

We may also consider the case of a body striking another at rest. The impulse is not transmitted in a straight line; it is divided between the two bodies. One receives a portion of the energy that moves in a curve directed toward the other, whilst the other receives another portion that moves in a contrary direction. The energy thus expended is not equal in both cases; it varies in intensity according to the speed of the striking body and the resistance of the body struck.

I am inclined to conclude that the answer to your question is this: the force cannot all be applied uniformly along a straight line. Some portion of it is directed toward a determinate object, and this object becomes its own centre of motion. The object's energy enters this motion, not from an instantaneous application of a uniform stress, but from the directional alteration of energy that follows the moving body's contact with another. The object is not wholly moved; it is altered in its motion.

This principle extends far beyond mere mechanics. It touches upon the nature of collision, the character of motion among bodies, and the intricate combinations of forces that shape our world.
