You have touched a matter I have long debated, and I find it rather compelling. Consider a body moving with a constant, uniform pace, which we might call c. If we apply a force sufficient to overcome that pace, then the body proceeds with a velocity equal to c. Should we persist with our application without change of direction, the velocity would be unchanged, and the object would never reach c.

But here we encounter a peculiar form of power: we cannot apply the whole of our force in the same direction. We must alter its direction by a certain amount. This is the principle I sought to establish: a force may not be applied in the same direction for ever. It must be applied at an angle, so to speak, or it will not have its due effect.

This reasoning applies with particular force to a body striking a fixed object at a uniform level. The momentum and energy we bring to bear upon that body are not uniform in application; they are spent in altering its direction and motion. What we recover may be a different kind of energy, energy of motion, energy of vibration, or some other form we cannot measure directly because our bodies cannot match it.

I therefore conclude that the energy expended in directing a body from a fixed position is not lost; it enters a new mode of action, finding its expression in the body's momentum and moving energy. The body may not attain the velocity c; it reaches a higher level of speed, or perhaps a different path altogether. The energy has been absorbed into this new form.

This principle serves admirably in physics: a moving body does not remain forever at the same level; it may travel a different path. The same force applied at a different angle may produce its own specific effect.

You have touched a matter of considerable importance to the science of optics, one I have long debated with those who favour a different view from that of our present philosophers.

I am inclined to think that light moves much swifter than our present theories allow. We speak of a ray as a succession of waves, yet we find that in the same instant of time many rays arrive at different directions from the same object. This suggests that light does not move with the same velocity in all inertial frames, but rather varies in speed throughout its passage.

A train moving at steady pace compared with the ground upon which it rests will produce a succession of different impulses upon the moving particles of air and water. The foremost strike reaches the ground surface, the second strikes the front end, the third strikes the back end, and so forth. Each strike arrives at the same moment for the observer situated midway between the two.

Now consider an observer standing motionless at the midpoint and observing the two flashes simultaneously: he sees the front strike arriving first, the back strike arriving second. This is not a coincidence; it indicates that the two strike together in the ground frame, and that the same impression of a quick, sharp, violent discharge reaches him.

We must therefore reconsider our assumptions: we suppose light moving according to a fixed law, and we account for only uniform motion in inertial frames. The train observer's position with regard to the two strikes determines whether he perceives simultaneously the two discharges. I am inclined to conclude that light does not move according to a fixed law of motion alone, but according to the particular constitution of the medium through which it passes.

We may therefore conclude that a train moving at constant speed relative to the ground shall generate two simultaneous discharges of equal energy and violence, arriving at the same moment or a little later in the ground frame. The flash seen in the front and back ends of the train together is therefore the more powerful and the more recent of the two.

We may also conclude that the same flash arriving from different directions in the ground frame may arrive at different moments. The ground observer standing at the midpoint places himself in a position where he sees both simultaneously, and draws the inference that the two strike together. This observation confirms that light moves at different rates in different inertial frames, and that we must abandon our theory of uniform motion in every inertial frame.

I find myself drawn to this notion: light moves at different speeds in different frames, and when two strikes arrive simultaneously in the same frame, the two forces converge and the observer sees a single, sudden, violent effect. This is precisely what we witness in nature's effects: a storm arrives at precisely the same moment in two different directions, and the observer sees only one flash. The analogy drawn from motion is thus corroborated: light moves at different rates in different frames, and these rates correspond to the different species of inertial framework through which it passes.

You have touched a point I have long debated with myself, and I am somewhat perplexed by it.

I am inclined to think that acceleration and gravity are not two separate forces, but rather two effects of one and the same motion. I find this difficult to hold: a body falling from a great height cannot simply swing downward by its own motion alone. It must overcome an accelerated motion during its descent. We observe this with our own bodies: a swift walk downhill moves us more swiftly than a slow walk on a level floor; we are conscious of this change.

We observe also that a falling body, once it reaches the ground, strikes the surface with a force equal to that it acquired in its ascent. This force we transfer to the earth: it presses the body upon the surface, and we feel the pressure as we stoop or fall. We do not weigh downward; we feel downward.

I am inclined to conclude that a body in motion, when it reaches the ground, offers a change in shape and time that the surrounding space receives and resists. This change is not a force acting on mass, but rather a change in our perception of weight and distance. We infer from this that gravity is not a separate force but a kind of stress in time and space.

Nowhere do I find that acceleration alone explains gravity. It is the combination of these two that produces our perception of weight. The light beam does not bend because gravity alone would bend it; it bends because accelerated motion exerts its force upon something already in motion.

We conclude, therefore, that acceleration and gravity are physically equivalent: they are the same motion of space, modified by time and space. The answer I would give is: acceleration and gravity are not two forces acting on mass alone, but rather two expressions of the same motion modified by the medium through which it operates.

1. Imagine a sealed elevator far out in space, away from planets and stars.
2. If the elevator accelerates upward at 9.8 m/s^2, an observer inside would feel pressed to the floor exactly as a person standing on Earth feels their weight.
3. If the observer dropped an object inside the elevator, it would fall to the floor just as it does in an ordinary gravitational field.
4. This suggests that, at least locally, acceleration and gravity are physically equivalent.

Now consider light:
5. Suppose a beam of light enters one side of the accelerating elevator and travels straight across toward the other side.
6. While the light is crossing, the elevator continues accelerating upward.

You have touched a matter of considerable importance in our present discussions of light and gravitation.

I am inclined to think that acceleration and gravity are not two distinct forces acting upon different substances, but rather two manifestations of the same fundamental phenomenon: the motion of a body through space. When a body moves in a direction other than straight forward, it is not pushing against something at a distance; it is exerting its own forward thrust upon the form of space it occupies. This change in shape is what we perceive when we observe a stone or a bullet descending.

I am inclined to infer, therefore, that acceleration and gravity are equivalent in effect. The elevator does not push a separate particle; it pulls in a direction we have described: upward and forward. This downward acceleration corresponds to a change in space form: the body shifts from its ordinary static position to one of relative motion, which we perceive as the fall or the collision.

We can thus reduce our explanation to this: acceleration and gravity are not distinct forces acting upon different substances, but rather two aspects of the same physical process: a change in space and time that produces pressure, rotation, or acceleration upon those bodies which share the same motion. The light does not move away from the accelerating body; it bends along the line of motion, and thus exerts its force.

This makes for our conclusion: acceleration and gravity are equivalent expressions of the same physical stress upon the body, and by analogy with other phenomena, we may conclude that gravity operates in an accelerating frame, and light follows the same pattern of change.

We may therefore assert that light and gravity are not two distinct forces but two aspects of the same physical motion: acceleration and retardation are reversed forms of the same action upon space.

You have touched a matter I have long debated, and I am inclined toward a view that light is not confined to particles of matter but rather to the change of space. When a body moves through our atmosphere or into the ether, that change is communicated to the medium around us. This medium behaves like a soft body yielding to an action that we call acceleration. We find this in every instance: a stone thrown upward, a falling body, a falling leaf, a stone dropped from a height. The motion is transmitted as a force that draws another body toward the same mass, precisely as we observe in the accelerated elevator or the fall of a stone.

I suspect our reasoning leads us to this: acceleration creates a stress in the medium; that stress produces a change in space. The stress then acts upon the light, or rather upon the medium that responds to it. We observe this in every phenomenon: a string stretched by a pull acts upon a weight at a distance, a spring exerts its force at a distance, our bodies respond to change in distance. We do not find our weight altered by particles of matter; we find space altered and time altered.

Moreover, we find this principle universal: change of space generates a pull through whatever instrument we employ. The hand that lifts a heavy stone from the ground produces pressure upon the floor in proportion to the space passed through. We cannot measure the mass of the stone; we measure the pull it exerts against the floor. So with light: acceleration acts upon the medium that yields to it, transforming shape and time, and we find this transformation reflected in every phenomenon we observe.

This suggests we are not observing two forces simultaneously pulling upon different substances, but one force operating upon a different body according to the character of that body's motion. The acceleration is not a force pulling particles of matter; it is a change in the medium's shape and time, which we perceive in the light bent or moving downward. It is a consequence of motion's transformation, not an immediate grip upon matter.

We are thus compelled to conclude: acceleration and gravity are not opposed but equivalent forces, and the light we observe mirrors this truth precisely in our experiment.

You have touched a point I have long debated with myself, and I am somewhat perplexed by it.

I am inclined to think that we ought not consider gravity as a single force acting upon every body independently, but rather as a consequence of the structure of space and time. When a body falls, a certain acceleration is produced. This acceleration is not a fixed constant, but varies according to the weight of the body, and the velocity with which it strikes the ground. It is not uniform; it is greater in one direction than another.

We might suppose that gravity acts in concert with this acceleration, producing a gradual disappearance of weight during the descent. The body does not fall alone; it is carried along by the force of its own motion, and the weight of that motion is gradually extinguished. This extinction is not a matter of an isolated individual effort; it is the result of the balance of forces: the weight of the whole body, the velocity of the body, the distance fallen, and the time of fall.

Now, I find myself inclined toward a more modest view: we should not suppose gravity to be an unalterable principle acting alone upon every body indifferently. It is a matter of motion, shift, and the relation between weight and velocity during the fall. When our body falls, this shifting effect is reversed; the weight is not uniformly removed, but is diminished by the accelerated motion.

This seems to me a more reasonable account of the phenomenon than our older theories suggest. The mind ought not to leap to the notion that gravity is an unalterable force acting upon every individual body. It is rather an effect of the structure of space and time, and we ought to reason about it accordingly.

The velocity acquired during the fall varies with the body's distance from the ground or other support. It is not uniform; it is not a constant pull of equal weight; it is a shift in direction and energy.
